Separability in Büchi VASS and Singly Non-Linear Systems of Inequalities
Pascal Baumann, Eren Keskin, Roland Meyer, Georg Zetzsche

Abstract
The ω \omega -regular separability problem for Büchi VASS coverability languages has recently been shown to be decidable, but with an 𝖤𝖷𝖯𝖲𝖯𝖠𝖢𝖤 \mathsf{EXPSPACE} lower and a non-primitive recursive upper bound—the exact complexity remained open. We close this gap and show that the problem is 𝖤𝖷𝖯𝖲𝖯𝖠𝖢𝖤 \mathsf{EXPSPACE} -complete. A careful analysis of our complexity bounds additionally yields a 𝖯𝖲𝖯𝖠𝖢𝖤 \mathsf{PSPACE} procedure in the case of fixed dimension ≥ 1 \geq 1 , which matches a pre-established lower bound of 𝖯𝖲𝖯𝖠𝖢𝖤 \mathsf{PSPACE} for one dimensional Büchi VASS. Our algorithm is a non-deterministic search for a witness whose size, as we show, can be suitably bounded. Part of the procedure is to decide the existence of runs in VASS that satisfy certain non-linear properties. Therefore, a key technical ingredient is to analyze a class of systems of inequalities where one variable may occur in non-linear (polynomial) expressions. These so-called singly non-linear systems (SNLS) take the form 𝑨 ⁡ ( x ) ⋅ 𝒚 ≥ 𝒃 ⁡ ( x ) \bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x) , where 𝑨 ⁡ ( x ) \bm{A}(x) and 𝒃 ⁡ ( x ) \bm{b}(x) are a matrix resp. a vector whose entries are polynomials in x x , and 𝒚 \bm{y} ranges over vectors in the rationals. Our main contribution on SNLS is an exponential upper bound on the size of rational solutions to singly non-linear systems. The proof consists of three steps. First, we give a tailor-made quantifier elimination to characterize all real solutions to x x . Second, using the root separation theorem about the distance of real roots of polynomials, we show that if a rational solution exists, then there is one with at most polynomially many bits. Third, we insert the solution for x x into the SNLS, making it linear and allowing us to invoke standard solution bounds from convex geometry. Finally, we combine the results about SNLS with several techniques from the area of VASS to devise an 𝖤𝖷𝖯𝖲𝖯𝖠𝖢𝖤 \mathsf{EXPSPACE} decision procedure for ω \omega -regular separability of Büchi VASS.

1
Introduction

Vector addition systems with states (VASS) are one of the most popular and well-studied models of concurrent systems. A
d
-dimensional VASS consists of finitely many control states and
d
counters. Transitions between control states can increment or decrement the
d
counters, but importantly, one can only take a transition if the new counter values remain non-negative.

Separability problems

In recent years, a strong focus of the research on VASS was on
separability problems

[
18
,
2
,
11
,
32
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
5
,
6
,
16
,
17
]
. Here, we label the transitions of the input VASS
\mathcal{V}_{1}
,
\mathcal{V}_{2}
by letters, which gives rise to languages
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. Then, we ask whether there exists a language
S
, from some class
\mathcal{S}
of allowed separators, such that
L_{1}\subseteq S
and
L_{2}\cap S=\emptyset
. Here,
\mathcal{S}
is typically the class of regular languages.

An important motivation for studying separability problems is that
separators can be viewed as certificates for disjointness, and thus the non-existence of a run in the product of
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
. Such certificates are crucial for understanding safety verification for infinite-state systems, where the difficult part is to prove the non-existence of a run (the existence of a run is usually easy to show).
In particular, certificates for non-existence are often the ingredient that is conceptually hardest to come by. For example, in the case of reachability in VASS, the KLM decomposition
[
27
,
19
,
20
,
23
]
and Leroux’s Presburger-definable inductive invariants
[
22
]
can be viewed as such certificates. Regular separators could play a similar role in alternative approaches to reachability.

In addition to understanding certificates, the recent attention on separability has led to other applications. For example, work on separability by bounded languages has led to a general framework to address unboundedness problems for VASS
[
8
]
. Moreover, separability results were used in an algorithm for deciding inclusion between unambiguous VASS
[
7
]
.

With the recent contribution by Keskin and Meyer
[
17
]
(together with earlier decidability results for subclasses and variants
[
2
,
11
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
5
,
6
]
), proving regular separability decidable for (finite-word) VASS, the
decidability
status of regular separability has largely been settled. However, concerning
complexity
, regular separability is far from understood, with few results: So far, the only exact complexity results are
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness for (succintly represented) one-dimensional VASS
[
9
]
,
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-completeness for VASS coverability languages
[
10
]
, and Ackermann-completeness for VASS reachability languages
[
17
]
.

Büchi VASS

A particularly challenging problem is (
\omega
-)regular
separability in Büchi VASS
[
2
]
. In a Büchi VASS
\mathcal{V}
, the
language
L(\mathcal{V})
consists of
infinite words
induced by runs that visit
some final state infinitely often. As demonstrated by Baumann, Meyer, and
Zetzsche
[
2
]
, Büchi VASS behave quite differently in terms
of regular separability from their finite-word counterpart, coverability
languages of VASS
[
10
]
. Nevertheless, Baumann, Meyer, and Zetzsche
proved decidability of regular separability for Büchi VASS
[
2
]
.
However, the complexity remained open: Their algorithm requires at least
Ackermannian time (because it constructs Karp-Miller graphs),
and the only known lower bound is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
.

Challenge: Non-linear constraints

Improving the complexity established in
[
2
]
is challenging due to the characterization of inseparability there:
Inseparability is equivalent to the existence of a constellation of runs, called an
inseparability
flower
, that must satisfy a
non-linear constraint
, meaning a constraint that is not
expressible in linear arithmetic (i.e. first-order logic of
(\mathbb{Z};+,<,0,1)
or

(\mathbb{Q};+,<,0,1)
). Essentially, such a flower is a triple
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

of cyclic runs such that (among other linear inequalities) the counter effect
of the combined run
\alpha\beta\gamma
is a scalar multiple of the counter effect of just
\alpha
.
In other words, we are looking for runs with effects
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that

\exists x\in\mathbb{Q}\colon\bm{v}=x\cdot\bm{u}\ .

(1)

Detecting runs with such constraints is
difficult: There are powerful generic
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
algorithms for detecting runs
that satisfy unboundedness conditions
[
12
]
,
linear constraints
[
1
]
, or variants
of computation tree logic (CTL)
[
4
]
. However, condition
(
1
) falls in neither of those categories.

In fact, we are not aware of any algorithmic approach to solving systems of
linear inequalities with constraints of type (
1
) (let alone
inside algorithms for VASS). There is a result by Gurari & Ibarra
[
13
]

showing that integral feasibility of systems of equalities
\bm{A}\cdot\bm{y}=\bm{b}(x)
can be decided in
\mathsf{NP}
, where
\bm{b}(x)
is a vector containing in each component a
quotient of polynomials in
x
. However, these do not seem to capture
(
1
): By moving the denominators from
\bm{b}(x)
to the left-hand side, one obtains equations
where every variable from
\bm{y}
is multiplied with the same polynomial over
x
. However, for (
1
), we need to multiply a
subset
of the linear variables (namely, those in
\bm{u}
) with a polynomial (namely,
x
).
The same is
true for the logic of
almost linear arithmetic
due to
Weispfenning
[
33
]
, whose existential fragment is also
solvable in
\mathsf{NP}
. Here, the definable sets are finite unions of solution sets
of Gurari & Ibarra.

Furthermore, it is not even clear how to detect inseparability flowers by invoking
reachability in VASS (even though this would only yield an Ackermann upper
bound): To some extent, algorithms for reachability permit non-linear
constraints—for example, using standard tricks, it is decidable whether one
can reach a configuration with counter values
(m,n)
such that
n\leq 2^{m}
.
However, the condition in (
1
) does not even seem to be
captured by such methods.

Contribution

Our main result is that regular separability in
Büchi VASS is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-complete, and
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete in fixed
dimension
\geq 1
. The key technical ingredient is a method that we expect to
be of independent interest: We develop a procedure for solving systems of
linear inequalities with a single non-linear variable, which we call

singly non-linear systems of inequalities
(SNLS). We use our results
about SNLS to show that if an inseparability witness exists, then there is one
where all runs have at most doubly exponential length, yielding an
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}

procedure. In fixed dimension, we obtain singly exponential bounds, leading to a
\mathsf{PSPACE}
procedure.

Step I: Singly non-linear systems of inequalities

Intuitively, a singly non-linear system of inequalities (SNLS) is a system of inequalities that is linear in all but one variable. This means, there is one variable
x
that may appear in arbitrary polynomials, but all others can only occur linearly. More precisely, an SNLS is a system of inequalities of the form

\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x),\quad\bm{y}\geq\bm{0}\ ,

(2)

where
\bm{A}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m\times n}
is an
m\times n
matrix over the ring
\mathbb{Z}[x]
of integer polynomials in
x
,
\bm{b}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m}
is a vector of polynomials from
\mathbb{Z}[x]
, and
\bm{y}
ranges over
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
. Notice that here indeed,
x
can be freely multiplied with itself and other variables, whereas the expression on the left-hand side must be linear in each component of
\bm{y}
.

Our main result about SNLS is that if a system as in (
2
) has a solution
(x,\bm{y})\in\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, then it has a solution where all numbers (numerators and denominators) are bounded exponentially in the description size of
\bm{A}(x)
and
\bm{b}(x)
, even if numbers in the description are encoded in binary. This implies in particular that feasibility of SNLS is in
\mathsf{NP}
.

In the proof, we first show that the set of all
x\in\mathbb{Q}
for which there is a
solution
(x,\bm{y})
can be described by a Boolean combination
\Phi
of
polynomial constraints of the form
p(x)\geq 0
, for polynomials

p\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
. This amounts to a quantifier elimination procedure for a class of first-order formulas in the ordered field
(\mathbb{Q};+,\cdot,<,0,1)
. This is perhaps surprising, since this structure does not admit quantifier elimination in general
[
25
, Theorem 2]
.

Let us give a geometric explanation how we arrive at the constraints
\Phi(x)
:
For each choice of
x
, the SNLS
\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x),~\bm{y}\geq\bm{0}

defines a polyhedron. It is a standard fact in convex geometry that
such a polyhedron has a point on a minimal face, and moreover this point can be
expressed as the inverse of a submatrix of
\bm{A}(x)
multiplied with
\bm{b}(x)
.
This expression can then be plugged back into

\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)
to obtain a set of polynomial constraints on
x
,
subject to a particular determinant being non-zero. The latter non-zero
condition can as well be expressed as a polynomial constraint.

We then show that
\Phi
has a small solution: In one case, a rational root of
one of the polynomials
p
is a solution—these can be bounded by the

Rational Root Theorem
. The other case is that the solution
x
lies
strictly between two roots
r_{1}<r_{2}
of participating polynomials. But then one
can observe that any rational number between those roots is a solution (if
no other root lies between
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
). Using the
Root
Separation Theorem
(specifically, Rump’s Bound
[
28
]
), which lower-bounds the size
of such intervals
(r_{1},r_{2})
, we can then conclude that such an interval must
contain a rational number with small numerator and denominator.

Once we exhibit a small
x
, we can plug it into
\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)

to obtain a system of linear inequalities.
Then we use standard bounds to obtain a small (i.e. exponential)
solution
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
. It should be noted that while our result about SNLS
concerns rational solutions, we apply it in the case where
\bm{b}(x)\geq\bm{0}
,
which means a rational solution can be turned into an integral solution by
multiplying a common denominator.

Step II: Rackoff-like bounds

After establishing the solution bound for SNLS, we use this result in the context of Büchi VASS to show the existence of inseparability witnesses that are small, i.e. consist of runs that are at
most doubly exponential in length. Here, we use an adaptation of the Rackoff
technique
[
29
]
similar to the proofs of
Habermehl
[
14
]
and Atig &
Habermehl
[
1
]
.
In

[
1
]
, it is shown that runs satisfying
(restricted) linear inequalities can be detected in
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
.
For this, they use a Rackoff-style induction to bound the length of such runs.
We devise a similar Rackoff-style induction to work with SNLS instead of
ordinary linear inequalities.
Different compared to the earlier works is the fact that our witnesses contain
\omega
-counters, which may change when invoking the induction hypothesis.
Moreover, we need to use a result of Demri on selective unboundedness
[
12
, Theorem 4.6]
(in
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
in the general case and
\mathsf{PSPACE}
in fixed dimension) to check the coverability of our witnesses.

2
Preliminaries

Büchi VASS

A
Büchi vector addition system with states (Büchi VASS)

of dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
over an alphabet
\Sigma

is a tuple
\mathcal{V}=(Q,q_{0},\Sigma,T,F)
.
It consists of a finite set of states
Q
,
an initial state
q_{0}\in Q
,
a set of final states
F\subseteq Q
,
and a finite set of transitions
T\subseteq\ Q\times\Sigma^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times Q
.
The size of the Büchi VASS is
|\mathcal{V}|:=|Q|+|F|+\sum_{(q,w,\delta,q^{\prime})\in T}\big(|w|+|\!|\delta|\!|_{2}\big)
.
By
|\!|\delta|\!|_{2}
, we mean the size of the binary encoding of
\delta
.
Since we only consider Büchi VASS in this paper, we often simply call them VASS.
If
d=0
, we call
\mathcal{V}
a
Büchi automaton
.

The semantics of the Büchi VASS is defined over its
configurations
, which are elements of
Q\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
.
The
initial configuration
of
\mathcal{V}
is
(q_{0},\bm{0})
.
We lift the transitions of the Büchi VASS to a relation over configurations

\rightarrow\ \subseteq\ Q\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\Sigma^{*}\times Q\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
as follows:

(q,\bm{m})\xrightarrow{w}(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})
if there is
(q,w,\delta,q^{\prime})\in T
such that
\bm{m}^{\prime}=\bm{m}+\delta
.
A
run
of the Büchi VASS is a (possibly infinite) sequence of configurations of the form

\sigma=(p_{0},\bm{m}_{0})\xrightarrow{w_{1}}(p_{1},\bm{m}_{1})\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\cdots
.

A run
\sigma
is
accepting
if it starts from the initial configuration
and visits final states infinitely often,
meaning there are infinitely many configurations
(q,\bm{m})
in
\sigma
with
q\in F
.
The run is said to be
labeled
by the word
w=w_{0}w_{1}\cdots
in
\Sigma^{\omega}
.
The
language

L(\mathcal{V})
of the Büchi VASS consists of all infinite
words that label an accepting run.

An infinite-word language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is called
regular

if it is accepted by a Büchi automaton.
As we only consider infinite-word languages, we just call them languages.

Arithmetic

Our approach to regular separability in Büchi VASS rests on a result about solutions to singly non-linear systems of inequalities. This also requires some terminology.

We define the integers, rationals, polynomials, and matrices together with the operations we need to perform on them.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer.
Its size
|\!|a|\!|_{2}=|\mathit{bin}(a)|
is the length of its binary encoding.
We also use
|\!|a|\!|_{1}
to denote the size of the unary encoding.
This is the absolute value plus an extra bit for the sign.
A polynomial with integer coefficients
p\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
is a sum
\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}x^{i}
with
a_{0},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a_{k}\neq 0
if
k>0
.
We define
|\!|p|\!|_{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}|\!|a_{i}|\!|_{1}
and similar for
|\!|p|\!|_{2}
.
The
degree
of the polynomial is
\deg(p)=k
, its
maximal coefficient
is
\maxc(p)=\max_{i\in[0,k]}|\!|a_{i}|\!|_{1}
.
Note that
|\!|p|\!|_{1}\leq(\deg(p)+1)\cdot\maxc(p)
.
A real number
r\in\mathbb{R}
with
p(r)=0
is called a
root
of the polynomial.
Let
\mathit{S}
be a set with a size function
|\!|-|\!|
defined on it.
We consider matrices
\bm{A}\in\mathit{S}^{m\times n}
over
\mathit{S}
,
and define their size
|\!|\bm{A}|\!|
by summing up the sizes of the entries.
We use
\mathit{row}(\bm{A})=m
and
\mathit{col}(\bm{A})=n
.
When
\mathit{S}=\mathbb{Z}[x]
, we also use
\deg(\bm{A})
for the highest degree of a polynomial in
\bm{A}
and
\maxc(\bm{A})
for the maximal coefficient of a polynomial in
\bm{A}
.
Pairs
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathit{S}\times\mathit{S}
form a special case with size
|\!|(s_{1},s_{2})|\!|=|\!|s_{1}|\!|+|\!|s_{2}|\!|
.
In particular, a rational number
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
is a pair
\mathit{t}=\frac{a}{b}
of integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|\!|\mathit{t}|\!|_{1}=|\!|a|\!|_{1}+|\!|b|\!|_{1}
, and similar for
|\!|\mathit{t}|\!|_{2}
.

We perform addition
a+b
and multiplication
a\cdot b
among integers, rationals, polynomials, and matrices.
These operations can be executed in time polynomial in
|\!|a|\!|_{2}+|\!|b|\!|_{2}
.
The same holds for the comparison
a\geq b
among integers and rationals.
We also add, multiply, and compare integers and rationals with
-\infty
and
\infty
.
The definitions are as expected.

3
Main results

A language
\mathit{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is said to
separate
languages
\mathit{L}_{1},\mathit{L}_{2}\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
, if
\mathit{L}_{1}\subseteq\mathit{R}
and
\mathit{R}\cap\mathit{L}_{2}=\emptyset
.
We call
\mathit{L}_{1}
and
\mathit{L}_{2}

regular separable
, denoted by
\mathit{L}_{1}\mathop{|}\mathit{L}_{2}
, if there is a separator
\mathit{R}
that is a regular language.
The problem we address is the
regular separability problem
for Büchi VASS:

Given

Two VASS
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
over some alphabet
\Sigma
.

Question

Does
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{1})\mathop{|}\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{2})
hold?

We also consider the variants of this problem where the inputs are of fixed dimension:
For a fixed number
d\in{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}
, the
d
-dimensional regular separability problem
for Büchi VASS is the same problem as above, except that the input VASS
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
are restricted to be of dimension at most
d
.
Our first main result is the following:

Theorem 1
.

The regular separability problem for Büchi VASS is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-complete. Moreover, the
d
-dimensional regular separability problem is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete for all
d\geq 1
.

As mentioned above, the proof is based on a small model property for what we call singly non-linear systems of inequalities. We expect this result to be of independent interest.
Formally, a
singly non-linear system (SNLS)
is a system of inequalities of the form

\displaystyle\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)\wedge\bm{y}\geq\bm{0}\ .

Here,
\bm{A}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m\times n}
is an
m\times n
matrix over the set of polynomials with integer coefficients in variable
x
,
and
\bm{b}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m}
is a vector of polynomials.
We also write an SNLS as
\mathcal{S}=(\bm{A}(x),\bm{b}(x))
, or
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
to emphasize the variables.
A
solution
to
\mathcal{S}
is a pair
(\mathit{t},\bm{s})\in\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}^{n}
that satisfies
\bm{A}(\mathit{t})\cdot\bm{s}\geq\bm{b}(\mathit{t})\wedge\bm{s}\geq\bm{0}
.
If a solution exists, we call the system
feasible
.

Our second main result is a bound on the size of least solutions.

Theorem 2
.

If the SNLS
\mathcal{S}
is feasible, then it has a solution
(\mathit{t},\bm{s})
, where all components of
\bm{s}
have the same denominator, with
|\!|\mathit{t}|\!|_{1},|\!|\bm{s}|\!|_{1}\in(\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2}\cdot\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{4})}
.

Theorem

2
implies that a feasible system
\mathcal{S}
always has a solution of size at most singly exponential in
|\!|\mathcal{S}|\!|_{1}
.
This gives an upper bound on the complexity of feasibility.

Corollary 3
.

Feasibility of SNLS is in
\mathsf{NP}
.

The reader may have noted that SNLS are more general than the non-linear systems we are confronted with when checking separability.
There are at least two arguments in support of the generalization.
First, non-linearity is not well-understood, and we believe a class of systems that admits an efficient algorithm for checking feasibility will find its applications.
Second, the generalization only adds little complexity to the proof or, phrased differently, the special case already needs most considerations.

Organization

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In
Section

4
, we prove
Theorem

2
and in
Section

5
, we show
Theorem

1
.

4
Singly Non-Linear Systems

In this section, we prove
Theorem

2
.

Some notation

By
\bm{A}(\mathit{t})
or
\mathit{eval}(\bm{A}(x),\mathit{t})
we mean the matrix with rational entries that results from
\bm{A}(x)
by evaluating all polynomials at
\mathit{t}
.
Let
\bm{A}\in R^{n\times n}
be a square matrix over some ring
R
. In our exposition, we will consider matrices over the rings
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\mathbb{Z}[x]
.
We write
\det(\bm{A})
for the determinant, and recall that if
R
is a field (such as
\mathbb{Q}
), then
\bm{A}
is invertible if and only if
\det(\bm{A})\neq 0
.
The
adjugate
(also called
classical adjoint
) of
\bm{A}
is the matrix
\adj(\bm{A})\in R^{n\times n}
with
\adj(\bm{A})[j,i]=(-1)^{i+j}\det(\bm{A}_{ij})
, where
\bm{A}_{ij}
is the matrix obtained from
\bm{A}
by removing the
i
-th row and the
j
-th column. It is well-known that then
\bm{A}\cdot\adj(\bm{A})=\det(\bm{A})\cdot\bm{I}
, where
\bm{I}
is the identity matrix in dimension
n
. In particular, if
\bm{A}
is invertible, its inverse can be computed as
\bm{A}^{-1}=\frac{\adj(\bm{A})}{\det(\bm{A})}

[
21
, Chapter XIII, Prop. 4.16]
.

In upper bound arguments, we will use the well-known Leibniz formula for
determinants, which says
\det(\bm{A})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sgn(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{A}[i,\sigma(i)]

[
21
, Chapter XIII, Prop.
4.6]
. Here,
S_{n}
is the set of all permutations of
[1,n]
and
\sgn(\sigma)\in\{-1,1\}
is the sign of
\sigma\in S_{n}
.

Bounding solutions

For the proof of
Theorem

2
, we proceed in two steps.
We first show that if an SNLS
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
is feasible, then we find a small rational
\mathit{t}
for
x
such that the system
\mathcal{S}(\mathit{t},\bm{y})
is feasible.
This system is the result of evaluating all polynomials in
\mathcal{S}
at
\mathit{t}
, and thus having only
\bm{y}
as the variables.

Lemma 4
.

If the SNLS
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
is feasible, then there is a number
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
with
|\!|\mathit{t}|\!|_{1}\in(\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{3})}

such that
\mathcal{S}(\mathit{t},\bm{y})
is feasible.

Lemma
4
is non-trivial and will occupy almost this entire section.
To explain our approach, note that the feasibility of
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
is equivalent to the feasibility of
\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
.
Our first step is to eliminate the quantifier and determine a new formula
\Phi(x)
in which
\bm{y}
no longer occurs and that is equivalent to the previous one over the rationals,
\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})\mathop{\models\hskip-8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\models$}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\Phi(x)
.
The equivalence says that for every
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have
\mathit{t}\models\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
if and only if
\mathit{t}\models\Phi(x)
.

The second step for
Lemma

4
is to show that if the new formula holds, then we find a small solution for
t
. To this end, we will apply the Root Separation Theorem, which provides a lower bound on the distance between distinct real roots of polynomials. After establishing
Lemma

4
, we obtain
Theorem

2
(at the end of this section) by taking the
t
provided by
Lemma

4
, and pair it with the
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, which must exist according to the quantifier elimination done in the first step of
Lemma

4
.

4.1
Quantifier Elimination

We show how to remove the quantifier from
\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
with a tailor-made quantifier elimination algorithm.
The fact that quantifier elimination is possible in this setting came as a surprise to us, given the non-linear nature and the setting of rationals. For example, the real closed field
(\mathbb{R};+,\cdot,<,0,1)
admits quantifier elimination by a classical result of Tarski
[
26
, Theorem 3.3.15]
, but this is not true for the ordered field
(\mathbb{Q};+,\cdot,<,0,1)
of rationals
[
25
, Theorem 2]
(see
[
26
, p. 71–72]
for a simple example). This means, there are first-order formulas over
(\mathbb{Q};+,\cdot,<,0,1)
that have no quantifier-free equivalent. However, we show that if we existentially quantify the linear variables in the formulas induced by SNLS, then those quantifiers can be eliminated.

The precise formulation of the result needs some notation.
A
lower bound constraint
has the form
p(x)\geq 0
or
p(x)>0
with
p\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
a polynomial with integer coefficients.
The formula
\Phi(x)
that we want to obtain takes the form

\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigwedge_{j\in J_{i}}\Phi_{i,j}(x)
, where the formulae
\Phi_{i,j}(x)
are lower bound constraints.
We call it a DNFLB, short for
disjunctive normal form with lower bound constraints as the literals
.
We may also omit
x
and write
\Phi
.
We use
\deg(\Phi)
and
\maxc(\Phi)
for the maximal degree resp. coefficient of a polynomial in
\Phi
.

Theorem 5
.

For every SNLS
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})
, there is a DNFLB
\Phi(x)
with
\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})\mathop{\models\hskip-8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\models$}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\Phi(x)
,
\deg(\Phi)\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))
, and
\maxc(\Phi)\in(\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{2})}
.

Since our intention is to bound the solutions
\mathit{t}
to variable
x
, the given estimations on the degree and the maximal coefficient suffice for us.
The proof actually gives an algorithm to compute
\Phi
which runs in time exponential in the dimension of
\mathcal{S}
, but we do not need the effectiveness here.
In the proof of
Theorem

5
, we will use a standard fact about polyhedra:

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
\bm{D}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
and
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}
. If the system

\bm{D}\cdot\bm{x}\geq\bm{c}
has a solution in
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, then there is a solution

\bm{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
that also satisfies
\bm{D}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{s}=\bm{c}^{\prime}
, where
(\bm{D}^{\prime},\bm{c}^{\prime})
is
a subset of the rows of
(\bm{D},\bm{c})
such that
\rank(\bm{D}^{\prime})=\rank(\bm{D})
.

Proof.

By well-known decomposition theorems about polyhedra, a polyhedron

P=\{\bm{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\mid\bm{D}\cdot\bm{s}\geq\bm{c}\}
is non-empty if and only if it has
a non-empty minimal face
[
31
, Theorem 8.5]
. Moreover,
minimal faces can be characterized as exactly the sets of the form

\{\bm{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\mid\bm{D}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{s}=\bm{c}^{\prime}\}
, where
(\bm{D}^{\prime},\bm{c}^{\prime})
is a subset of
the rows of
(\bm{D},\bm{c})
such that
\bm{D}^{\prime}
has the same rank as

\bm{D}

[
31
, Theorem 8.4]
.
∎

We are ready to prove
Theorem

5
:

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})=\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)\wedge\bm{y}\geq\bm{0}
.
To fix the dimension, let
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m\times n}
.
We can equivalently write the SNLS as
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,\bm{y})=\bm{D}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{c}(x)
with

\bm{D}(x)\ =\ \left(\begin{aligned} &\bm{A}(x)\\
&\bm{I}_{n}\end{aligned}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{(m+n)\times n}\qquad\bm{c}(x)\ =\ \left(\begin{aligned} &\bm{b}(x)\\
&\bm{0}_{n}\end{aligned}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]^{m+n}\ ,

i.e. we glue the
n\times n
identity matrix
\bm{I}_{n}
to the bottom of
\bm{A}(x)
and extend
\bm{b}(x)
by
n
zeros.

Assume
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is feasible and the solution for
x
is
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
By
Lemma

6
and since
\bm{D}(\mathit{t})
has rank
n
, we can select a subset of
n
rows of
\bm{D}(\mathit{t})
and of
\bm{c}(\mathit{t})
such that the smaller system has a solution, even with equality. More formally, for any subset
R\subseteq[1,m+n]
, denote by
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})
(resp.
\bm{c}_{R}(\mathit{t})
) the matrix (resp. vector) obtained by selecting only the rows in
R
from
\bm{D}(\mathit{t})
(resp.
\bm{c}(\mathit{t})
). Then
Lemma

6
tells us that there is an
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
with
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})\cdot\bm{s}=\bm{c}_{R}(\mathit{t})
, where
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})
has rank
n
. In particular,
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})
is invertible and thus
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t}))\neq 0
. The fact that
\bm{D}_{R}(t)
is invertible allows us to write
\bm{s}=\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})^{-1}\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(\mathit{t})
, which will be key for our quantifier elimination.
The argumentation shows that for every
t\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\exists\bm{y}.\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(t,\bm{y})
is equivalent to the condition

\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\subseteq[1,m+n]\\
|R|=n\end{subarray}}\det(\bm{D}_{R}(t))\neq 0\ \wedge\ \bm{D}(t)\cdot\bm{D}_{R}(t)^{-1}\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(t)\geq\bm{c}(t)\ .

(3)

Here, of course, we only know that
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})^{-1}
exists when
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t}))\neq 0
.
To express (
3
) using polynomials, we employ the identity
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})^{-1}=\tfrac{\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t}))}{\det(\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t}))}
whenever
\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t})
is invertible (equivalently, whenever
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(\mathit{t}))\neq 0
). Thus, the set of all
\mathit{t}
with
\exists\bm{y}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathit{t},\bm{y})
can be defined by the following DNFLB
\Phi
:

\displaystyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\subseteq[1,m+n]\\
|R|=n\end{subarray}}~

\displaystyle\Big(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))>0~\wedge~\bm{D}(x)\cdot\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(x)\geq\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}(x)\Big)

(4)

\displaystyle\vee\Big(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))<0~\wedge~\bm{D}(x)\cdot\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(x)\leq\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}(x)\Big)\ ,

where indeed all conditions are of the form
p(x)\geq 0
or
p(x)>0
for some polynomials
p
.
Note that here, we distinguish the cases
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))<0
and
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))>0
because moving a negative
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
to the other side of the inequality changes
\geq
to
\leq
. Moreover, note that (in contrast to (
3
)) in the formulation (
4
), all terms are well-defined, independenly of whether the current choice of
R
makes
\bm{D}_{R}(x)
invertible or not.
To be precise, we obtain the DNFLB by subtracting the right-hand sides of the inequalities from the left-hand sides and multiplying the result by
-1
to invert the inequality where necessary.
The above form will suffice to give an estimate on the maximal degree and the maximal coefficient.

It is now clear that the coefficients (resp. degrees) appearing in
\Phi
are exponential (resp. polynomial) in the bitsize of
\mathcal{S}
. The precise bounds promised in the Theorem are straightforward to deduce from standard bounds on determinants, see
Appendix

A
for details.
∎

4.2
Root Separation

To show
Lemma

4
, it remains to be shown that any feasible DNFLB
\Phi(x)
has a solution that is
exponentially bounded. The key observation is that if
r
and
r^{\prime}
are
adjacent roots of a polynomial
p(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
and a constraint
p(x)\geq 0
or

p(x)>0
is satisfied for some
\mathit{t}
for
x
with
r<\mathit{t}<r^{\prime}
,
then any number
\mathit{t}^{\prime}
in the open interval
(r,r^{\prime})
will
also satisfy the constraint: The polynomial does not change its sign between

r
and
r^{\prime}
. Thus, we can think of
\mathbb{R}
as being split into (i) roots of
p

and (ii) intervals between roots of
p
(and the infinite intervals below the smallest and above the largest root).
Then whether
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfies
p(x)\geq 0
or
p(x)>0
only depends on
which of those parts of
\mathbb{R}
the number
\mathit{t}
belongs to. This remains true if we refine this
decomposition of
\mathbb{R}
according to
all
polynomials occurring in
\Phi
.

In order to construct rational numbers with small numerator and denominator in intervals
(r,r^{\prime})
, we will rely on a Root Separation Theorem, saying that polynomial roots are not too close. More specifically, we use Rump’s Bound
[
28
, Theorem 8.5.5]
:

Theorem 7

(Rump’s Bound
[
28
, Theorem 8.5.5]
)
.

Suppose
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
are distinct roots of a polynomial
p(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
with degree
d\in{\rm Nature}
. Then
|r-r^{\prime}|>(d^{d+1}(1+|\!|p(x)|\!|_{1})^{2d})^{-1}
.

We will also use an elementary fact about rational roots of integral polynomials. It is known as the Rational Root Theorem or Integral Root Test
[
21
, Chapter IV, Prop. 3.3]
:

Lemma 8

(Rational Root Theorem
[
21
, Chapter IV, Prop. 3.3]
)
.

Let
p(x)=c_{n}x^{n}+\cdots+c_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
be a polynomial. If
r=a/b
is a root of
p
with
a,b
co-prime, then
a
divides
c_{0}
and
b
divides
c_{n}
. In particular,
|a|,|b|\leq\maxc(p)
.

Finally, we need a standard bound on all real roots of a polynomial
[
28
, Corollary 8.3.2]
. This is known as Cauchy’s bound.

Lemma 9

(Cauchy’s Bound
[
28
, Corollary 8.3.2]
)
.

If
r\in\mathbb{R}
is a root of a polynomial
p\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
, then
|r|\leq 1+|\!|p|\!|_{1}
.

Let
r_{1}<\cdots<r_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
be all the real roots of polynomials occurring in
\Phi
.
Observe that if
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfies
\Phi(x)
and
\mathit{t}\in(r_{i},r_{i+1})
, then any
rational number in
(r_{i},r_{i+1})
must satisfy
\Phi(x)
, because none of the
polynomials in
\Phi
changes its sign between
r_{i}
and
r_{i+1}
. This allows
us to bound a rational solution, by distinguishing the following cases:

1.

Suppose
\Phi(x)
is satisfied by some rational root
r_{i}
of
p
in
\Phi
. Write
r_{i}=\tfrac{a}{b}
with
a,b
co-prime. Then the Rational Root Theorem (
Lemma

8
) implies
|a|,|b|\leq\maxc(p)
.

2.

Suppose
\Phi(x)
has a rational solution in some interval
(r_{i},r_{i+1})
. Since
r_{i}
and
r_{i+1}
are the roots of some polynomials
p,q
in
\Phi(x)
, as observed above, any rational number in
(r_{i},r_{i+1})
is also a solution to
\Phi(x)
. Note that
r_{i},r_{i+1}
are roots of
p(x)\cdot q(x)
and thus by
Theorem

7
, we have
|r_{i}-r_{i+1}|>\tfrac{1}{b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{Z}
that is exponentially bounded. Thus, there is an integer
a\in(br_{i},br_{i+1})
. Note that then
\tfrac{a}{b}
belongs to the interval
(r_{i},r_{i+1})
and thus satisfies
\Phi(x)
. Moreover, by the Cauchy Bound (
Lemma

9
), we also have an exponential bound
U\in\mathbb{R}
on
|r_{i}|,|r_{i+1}|
and thus on
|a|\leq|b|U
.

3.

Suppose
\Phi(x)
has a rational solution
\mathit{t}
outside of
[r_{1},r_{k}]
. If
\mathit{t}>r_{k}
then every rational number in
[r_{k},\infty)
is also a solution. Moreover, by
Lemma

9
, any rational number
\mathit{t}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{t}^{\prime}>1+|\!|p|\!|_{1}
for every polynomial
p
occurring in
\Phi
can be chosen, e.g.
\mathit{t}^{\prime}=2+c
, where
c=\max\{|\!|p|\!|_{1}\mid p
polynomial in
\Phi\}
. On the other hand, if
\mathit{t}<r_{1}
, then
\mathit{t}^{\prime}=-(2+c)
is a solution by an analogous argument.

This proves that any feasible
\Phi(x)
has a rational solution that is exponentially bounded, which is what we will use in our application to Büchi VASS. The precise bounds of
Lemma

4
are shown in
Section

A.2
.

Proof sketch for
Theorem

2

For showing

Theorem

2
, we can now use the fact that if
t\in\mathbb{Q}
admits a
solution
(t,\bm{s})
, then by our argument in the proof of
Theorem

5
,

\bm{s}^{*}:=\tfrac{\adj(D_{R}(t))}{\det(D_{R}(t))}\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(t)
is also a
solution, for some subset
R\subseteq[1,m+n]
. This means that we can apply the
bound on
t
and the bounds on
\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
and
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
established in
the proof of
Theorem

5
to bound the solution
\bm{s}^{*}
.
We can ensure that all components of
\bm{c}_{R}(t)
have the same denominator by increasing the bit size at most
\deg(\mathcal{S})
-fold.
If we compute
\bm{s}^{*}
starting from such a vector, we get an
\bm{s}^{*}
where all components have the same denominator.
Since the
entries in
\bm{D}_{R}
all appear in
\mathcal{S}
and it is well-known that the
determinant has polynomial bit size in the bit size of a matrix, it follows
that there exists a solution
(t,\bm{s})
of polynomial bit size.
The precise
bounds promised in
Theorem

2
are derived in

Section

A.3
.

5
\omega
-Regular Separability

We use the results from
Section

4
to prove
Theorem

1
.
Note that the lower bounds in
Theorem

1
easily follow from
[
2
]
. First, that paper already shows
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness of regular separability for one-dimensional Büchi VASS, which yields the
\mathsf{PSPACE}
lower bound for fixed dimension
\geq 1
. In fact, their argument also yields
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hardness in the general case: The full version
[
3
, Appendix E.1]
describes a simple reduction from intersection emptiness of one-dimensional VASS that accept by final state to regular separability of Büchi VASS, and the construction is the same in higher dimension.
This yields
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hardness of regular separability of Büchi VASS, since intersection emptiness of VASS of arbitrary dimension that accept by final state is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hard
[
24
]
.

It remains to prove the upper bounds in
Theorem

1
.
To adequately formulate our proofs, we need to introduce additional VASS-related concepts.

More on Büchi VASS

Let
\mathcal{V}=(Q,q_{0},\Sigma,T,F)
be a Büchi VASS.
Consider a (possibly infinite) run

\sigma=(p_{0},\bm{m}_{0})\xrightarrow{w_{1}}(p_{1},\bm{m}_{1})\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\cdots
of
\mathcal{V}
.
The sequence of transitions in
\sigma
is called a
path
and has the form

\rho=(p_{0},w_{1},\delta_{1},p_{1})(p_{1},w_{2},\delta_{1},p_{2})\ldots
.
If a path is finite and the source state of its first transition coincides with the target state of its last transition, then we call it a
loop
. Since a run is uniquely determined by the start configuration and its sequence of transitions, we also denote a run by
\sigma=(p_{0},\bm{m}_{0}).\rho
.
If
\sigma
is finite and
(p_{\ell},\bm{m}_{\ell})
is its last configuration, then we sometimes write

\sigma=(p_{0},\bm{m}_{0}).\rho.(p_{\ell},\bm{m}_{\ell})
to emphasize this.
The
effect

\delta(\rho)
of some finite path
\rho=(p_{0},w_{1},\delta_{1},p_{1})\ldots(p_{\ell-1},w_{\ell},\delta_{\ell},p_{\ell})
is the sum of all induced counter changes, formally
\delta(\rho)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\delta_{i}
.

Recall that configurations of the Büchi VASS
\mathcal{V}
are elements of the set
Q\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
.
We call the second component in a configuration the
counter valuation

and refer to the
i
-th entry as the
value of counter
i
.
For a configuration
\mathit{cf}
and a set of counters
I\subseteq[1,d]
we also use
\mathit{cf}[I]
to denote the counter valuation of
\mathit{cf}
restricted to the counters in
I
.
A configuration
(q,\bm{m})
is
coverable
in
\mathcal{V}
if there is a run starting in the initial configuration
(q_{0},\bm{0})
and reaching a configuration
(q,\bm{m}^{\prime})
with
\bm{m}^{\prime}\geq\bm{m}
.
Here,
\geq
is defined component-wise.

Moreover we also consider a set of
extended configurations

Q\times{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d}
,
where
{\rm Nature}_{\omega}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
.
Here
\omega
is used to represent a counter value that has become unbounded.
For an extended configuration
(q,\bm{m})
we use
\omega(q,\bm{m})\subseteq[1,d]
to denote the
set of counters valued
\omega
in
\bm{m}
.
Comparisons and arithmetic operations between integer values and
\omega
behave as expected,
treating
\omega
as
\infty
.
Formally,
\omega\geq\omega
,
\omega\geq z
, and
\omega+z=\omega
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The
size
of an extended configuration is
|(q,\bm{m})|=\log_{2}|Q|+|\!|\bm{m}|\!|_{2}+d
, where the extra bit per counter encodes whether it has value
\omega
or not.
We also use the size of a unary encoding
|\!|(q,\bm{m})|\!|_{1}=|Q|+|\!|\bm{m}|\!|_{1}+d
.

The transition relation is also lifted to extended configurations in the expected manner.
Formally, for
(q,\bm{m})
,
(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in Q\times{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d}
we have

(q,\bm{m})\xrightarrow{w}(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})

if there is a transition
(q,w,\delta,q^{\prime})\in T
such that
\bm{m}^{\prime}=\bm{m}+\delta
,
where addition between elements of
{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is defined component-wise.
Furthermore, our definitions of runs, paths, loops, etc. carry over to
extended
versions over
the set of extended configurations in a straightforward way.
More precisely, an
extended run
is a sequence of extended configurations
\mathit{cf}_{1}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\mathit{cf}_{2}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\cdots
, an
extended path
is the underlying sequence of transitions of an extended run, and an
extended loop
is a finite extended path starting and ending in the same state.
To cover an extended configuration, intuitively, the
\omega
-counters need to become unbounded, and the remaining counters need to be covered.
Formally, an extended configuration
(q,\bm{m})
is
coverable
in
\mathcal{V}
if for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
there is a run starting in the initial configuration
(q_{0},\bm{0})
and reaching a configuration
(q,\bm{m}_{k})\in Q\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\bm{m}_{k}[j]\geq k
for every counter
j\in\omega(q,\bm{m})
and
\bm{m}_{k}[i]\geq\bm{m}[i]
for every counter
i\in[1,d]\setminus\omega(q,\bm{m})
.

Finally, we sometimes want to restrict only some counters of the VASS to stay non-negative.
In this case, we consider extended configurations in
Q\times\mathbb{Z}_{\omega}^{d}
,
where
\mathbb{Z}_{\omega}=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\omega\}
.
We say an extended run
\sigma=\mathit{cf}.\rho

remains non-negative
on counters

I\subseteq[1,d]
if
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}[I]\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{|I|}
for all extended configurations

\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
on
\sigma
.

Dyck Language

Towards the
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
upper bound of
Theorem

1
, a first step is to reduce
the separability problem to a variant where one language is fixed to the Dyck language.
The Dyck language
\mathit{D}_{n}
with
n
-letters is defined over the alphabet
\Sigma_{n}=\{a_{i},\bar{a}_{i}\;|\;i\in[1,n]\}
.
It contains those words
w
where, for every prefix
v
with
w=v.u
, we have at least as many letters
a_{i}
as
\bar{a}_{i}
.
Thus, the letters behave like VASS counters and, indeed, the Dyck language is accepted by a single-state VASS
\mathcal{D}_{n}
with
n
counters that increments the
i
-th counter upon seeing letter
a_{i}
and decrements the
i
-th counter upon seeing
\bar{a}_{i}
.
If a VASS is defined over the Dyck alphabet
\Sigma_{n}
, we also call it
n
-visible.
We will sometimes treat an
n
-visible VASS of dimension
d
as a
(d+n)
-dimensional VASS, and refer to the additional
n
counters as external.
Note that this amounts to forming the product with
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
Given a path
\rho
, we use
\varphi(\rho)
for the effect on the external counters in this product construction.
Moreover, we write
\delta\varphi(\rho)
to denote the combined effect on both internal and external counters, i.e. the
(d+n)
-dimensional vector
(\delta(\rho),\varphi(\rho))
.

To avoid an exponential blow-up, our reduction uses a variant of VASS whose transitions are labeled by compressed words. Essentially, the reduction takes
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
and produces a VASS
\mathcal{V}
that is a product of
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
. Moreover, it acts on its counters like
\mathcal{V}_{1}
; the input labels
of
\mathcal{V}
correspond to the counter updates of
\mathcal{V}_{2}
. Since the latter are binary-encoded, the new VASS will have binary encoded input words. Let us make this precise. A
label-compressed VASS
(lcVASS)
\mathcal{V}
is a VASS, where the transitions are of the form
(p,a^{m},\delta,q)\in Q\times\Sigma^{*}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times Q
, where
a\in\Sigma
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
is given in binary. Thus, for an lcVASS
\mathcal{V}
, we define its
size
as
|\mathcal{V}|=|Q|+|F|+\sum_{(q,a^{m},\delta,q^{\prime})\in T}(\log_{2}(m)+|\!|\delta|\!|_{2})
.
The reduction that fixes the Dyck language is captured by the following lemma.

Lemma 10

(
[
2
, Lemma 3.4]
)
.

Given
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
over
\Sigma
, we can compute in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{V}_{1}|+|\mathcal{V}_{2}|
an
n
-visible lcVASS
\mathcal{V}
so that
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{1})\mathop{|}\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{2})
if and only if
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathop{|}\mathit{D}_{n}
. Here,
n
is the dimension of
\mathcal{V}_{2}
.

The polynomial time bound is not mentioned in
[
2
]
, but the simple construction they use (from
[
11
]
) clearly implies this bound.
The latter separability problem
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathop{|}\mathit{L}(\mathit{D}_{n})
has been studied closely in
[
2
]
as well.
They first show that
\mathcal{V}
can be transformed so as to make the language
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})
pumpable.
For the resulting VASS, they show that
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathop{|}\mathit{L}(\mathit{D}_{n})
holds if and only if the so called Karp-Miller graph of
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V})
does not contain an inseparability witness.
Unfortunately, the transformation required for pumpability involves another Karp-Miller graph construction, and therefore does not fit into the space bound we aim for (said graph can be of Ackermannian size in the worst case).
Instead, we reformulate the witness.

Definition 11
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible
d
-dimensional VASS.
An
inseparability bloom
for
\mathcal{V}
is a tuple

\par}|=\log_{2}|Q|+|I|+|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\gamma|
.

A
stem for

is an extended run
\mathit{cf}.\sigma
of the product VASS
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n}
with the following properties: It ends in an extended configuration
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}=(q_{f},\bm{m})
for some
\bm{m}\in{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d+n}
with
\Omega\subseteq\omega(\mathit{cf}^{\prime})
, and the counters in
I\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
remain non-negative when executing
\sigma
from
\mathit{cf}
resp.
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
from
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
.

.
The flower is
coverable
if the extended configuration
\mathit{cf}
is coverable in the product VASS
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n}
.

The following can be derived from the results in
[
2
]
,
refer to
Appendix

B
for the details.

Lemma 12
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be
n
-visible.
We have
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathop{\not|}\mathit{D}_{n}
if and only if some inseparability flower is coverable.

Our main result is the following.
Note: the unary counter encoding strengthens the bound.

Theorem 13
.

If an inseparability flower is coverable in an
n
-visible lcVASS
\mathcal{V}
of dimension
d
, then there is one of size at most

.

Main algortihm

We now have all ingredients to formulate the algorithm that proves the upper bounds in Theorem
1
.
We first describe the
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
upper bound. Given
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
whose languages we wish to separate, we first compute the lcVASS
\mathcal{V}
using Lemma
10
.
This takes poly time.
The task is to check
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathop{|}\mathit{D}_{n}
, where
n
is the dimension of
\mathcal{V}_{2}
.
Using Lemma
12
, we have to find an inseparability flower for
\mathcal{V}
that is coverable.
Theorem
13
bounds the size of the flowers we have to consider.
We thus use non-determinism to find a flower of bounded size followed by a somewhat involved coverability check.
Savitch’s theorem
[
30
]
turns the non-deterministic algorithm into a deterministic one.

We detect a flower of bounded size as follows.
We first guess the final state
q_{f}\in F
and the partitioning of the counters
I\uplus\Omega
.
With this information, we can guess the stem.

Towards obtaining a suitable stem, we start by guessing an extended configuration
\mathit{cf}
, whose non-
\omega
-entries are at most doubly exponentially large.
We can store such configurations in exponential space.
If
\Omega\subseteq\omega(\mathit{cf})
fails, we abort.
We now guess a path
\sigma
of doubly exponential length from
\mathit{cf}
to a configuration
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
.
As we proceed, we store the length of the path, which only needs exponential space.
We abort, if one of the following happens while guessing
\sigma
: a counter from
I\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
becomes negative,
\sigma
becomes too long, or the last state on
\sigma
is different from
q_{f}
.
If we have not aborted until now, we have determined
\mathit{cf}.\sigma.\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
that may serve as a stem for a bloom with final state
q_{f}
and partition
I\uplus\Omega
.

Given the stem, we can finish the construction of the bloom
by guessing the cycles
\rho=\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
The reason we proceed in this order is the following.
The cycles are too long to be stored in exponential space.
Instead, we check the non-negativity required by a stem on-the-fly, while constructing the cycles.
To do so, we need the configuration
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
, which we can store upon finishing the guess of
\sigma
above.
While guessing the cycles, we store their length and the numbers
\delta(\rho)
and
\varphi(\rho)
.
It is readily checked that these numbers are bounded by

\displaystyle 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}}=2^{|\mathcal{V}|^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}}\ .

This means we can store them in exponential space.
We abort, if one of the following applies: an intermediate valuation becomes negative on
I\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
, the path becomes too long, or the last state is different from
q_{f}
.
We compute the operations and comparisons required by (i) to (iv) in
Definition

11
.
For (iv), we start with counter
d+1
and store the quotient
\frac{\varphi(\alpha)[d+1]+\varphi(\beta)[d+1]+\varphi(\gamma)[d+1]}{\varphi(\alpha)[d+1]}
as the rational number
\mathit{t}
.
As the numerator and denominator will be at most doubly exponential, we can store the number in exponential space.
For the remaining entries, we only perform the required comparison.
As noted above, they can be executed in polynomial time.
If a comparison fails, we reject.
If we have not rejected up to now, we have determined an inseparability flower while using space
|\mathcal{V}|^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}\leq 2^{|\mathcal{V}|\cdot\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}=2^{\mathit{poly}(|\mathcal{V}_{1}|+|\mathcal{V}_{2}|)}
.

It remains to check whether this flower is coverable.
There are two challenges.
First, since
\mathit{cf}
is extended, we have a combination of a simultaneous unboundedness and a coverability problem.
Second, the non-
\omega
-entries in
\mathit{cf}
may be doubly exponentially large.
We reduce the problem to simultaneous unboundedness, which is in
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
as shown by Demri
[
12
, Theorem 4.6(I)]
.
The reduction uses a simple gadget that subtracts the counter valuation to be covered and, if succcessful, makes a new target counter
j
unbounded.
In the end we check simultaneous unboundedness of
\omega(\mathit{cf})\cup\{j\}
.
To handle the large values, we utilize Lipton’s construction
[
24
]
,
which allows a VASS to simulate
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-computations.
As a remark, simultaneous unboundedness cannot be expressed by a polynomial-sized formula in Yen’s logic
[
34
]
, a fact that was first observed in
[
12
]
, which means we cannot just invoke the bounds in
[
1
]
.

For the
\mathsf{PSPACE}
upper bound, we merely need to observe that in fixed dimension, all our bounds on counter values become singly exponential. Moreover, for the gadget that subtracts counter values, we do not need the Lipton construction, as we can subtract exponentially bounded values directly using transitions. Finally, in fixed dimension, the simultaneous unboundedness check is also possible in
\mathsf{PSPACE}
, as shown by Demri
[
12
, Theorem 4.6(II)]
.

The remainder of the paper proves Theorem
13
.
SNLS help us deal with Constraint (iv).

6
Proof of Theorem
13

In this section we fix an
n
-visible lcVASS
\mathcal{V}=(Q,q_{0},\Sigma_{n},T,F)
.
Note that since
\mathcal{V}
is label-compressed, the effect on the external counters
\varphi(\rho)
for a path
\rho
is also compressed. This matches the effect
\delta(\rho)
, which is anyway encoded in binary.

The proof is Rackoff-like, and we explain the analogy as we proceed.
Like in Rackoff’s upper bound for coverability
[
29
]
, we reason over all (extended) configurations.
Unlike Rackoff, however, we do not look at short covering sequences from a given configuration, but rather at small flowers rooted in said configuration.
To bound the size while maximizing over all configurations, we measure each flower’s size without considering the configuration it is rooted in.
We therefore define the
flower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}\ =\ \max_{\mathit{cf}}\min\{|\sigma|+|\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1222.42pt{\vbox to1180.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-159.2006pt\lower-1488.80568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Theorem

13
is an immediate consequence of the following.

Lemma 14
.

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 2^{|\mathcal{V}|^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}}
.

Step I: From length bounds to flower size bounds

Before we prove
Lemma

14
, let us see how it implies
Theorem

13
. Notice that
Lemma

14
makes a statement about the lengths of the runs
\sigma
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
, whereas
Theorem

13
also promises a small starting configuration
\mathit{cf}
. Thus, it remains to construct a small starting configuration.

Proof of
Theorem

13
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{\prime}|\leq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
The extended configuration
\mathit{cf}
may not obey the desired bound.
Thus, we replace it by
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
defined by
\omega(\mathit{cf}^{\prime})=\omega(\mathit{cf})
and
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}[\mathit{j}]=\min\{\mathit{cf}[\mathit{j}],\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\}
for all
\mathit{j}\in I\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
.
Here, we keep the
\omega
-entries,
and for each non-
\omega
-counter, we take the value of
\mathit{cf}
unless it is larger than
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}
, in which case we truncate to this value.
We now claim that

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{\prime})
is still a coverable inseparability flower, and its size is at most doubly exponential (satisfying the desired bound).
Coverability is immediate by
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}\leq\mathit{cf}
.
We also have
|\!|\mathit{cf}^{\prime}|\!|_{1}\leq 2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{V}|)}}
.
To be an inseparability flower, we have to check that the counters whose values we truncated when moving from
\mathit{cf}
to
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
remain non-negative while executing
\sigma
, and also
\sigma.\rho
with
\rho=\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
This, however, is clear by the fact that
\sigma.\rho
has length at most
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
, and each transition can subtract at most
2^{|\mathcal{V}|}
tokens.
As we have this budget available in
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
, the run remains non-negative.
∎

In the remainder of the section, we prove Lemma
14
.
Similar to Rackoff’s proof for coverability, we generalize the notion of flowers to admit negative counter values.
Then we use an induction on the number of non-negative counters to establish a bound on the length of shortest generalized flowers.

\mathit{i}\in[0,d+n]
,

\par})
consisting of an
\mathit{i}
-stem and an inseparability bloom an
\mathit{i}
-inseparability flower
.
Note that a
(d+n)
-inseparability flower is an inseparability flower as in
Definition

11
.
We say the flower is
\mathit{b}
-bounded
with
\mathit{b}\in{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}
, if the counters in
([1,\mathit{i}]\cap I)\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
are bounded by
\mathit{b}
along
\mathit{cf}.\sigma.\rho
for all
\rho=\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
We wish to establish an estimate on the following function
f
, note that
\mathit{f}(d+n)=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par})\text{ is an $\mathit{i}$-inseparability flower}\}\ .

Step II: From value bounds to length bounds

Similar to Rackoff’s proof, we will bound
f(i+1)
in terms of
f(i)
, which will then yield the bound on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}=f(n+d)
. However, there is a key difference to Rackoff’s proof: When constructing runs that, in the first
i+1
coordinates stay non-negative, Rackoff argues that if such a run only uses counter values in
[0,b]
on the first
i+1
coordinates, then there is such a run of length at most
|Q|\cdot(b+1)^{i+1}
: whenever a combination of values in
[0,b]
(and a control state) repeats, we can cut out the infix in between. Hence,
value bounds yield length bounds
. Our setting requires a different argument: Simply cutting out infixes in
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
might spoil the properties (ii), (iii), and (iv) of
i
-inseparability flowers. Instead, we use our results on SNLS to obtain length bounds from value bounds.
The technique is similar to some other generalizations of Rackoff’s result by Yen
[
34
, Lemma 3.5]
Habermehl
[
14
, Lemma 3.2]
and Atig and Habermehl
[
1
, Lemma 5]
. However, the following proof also needs to work with non-linear constraints (which are also not present in Demri’s extension of Rackoff’s result
[
12
]
).

Lemma 15
.

be a
\mathit{b}
-bounded
\mathit{i}
-inseparability flower.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}=(q_{f},I,\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.
Let
\Omega
be the complement of
I
.
Let
\widehat{\mathit{cf}}
be the extended configuration reached by
\mathit{cf}.\sigma
.

Let
\mathit{D}=([1,\mathit{i}]\cap I)\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf})
be the counters we wish to keep non-negative.
By a
\mathit{D}
-loop
, we mean an extended run
\mathit{cf}_{1}.\tau.\mathit{cf}_{2}
of
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n}
where
\tau
is a loop,
\mathit{cf}_{1}[\mathit{D}]=\mathit{cf}_{2}[\mathit{D}]
, and
\mathit{cf}_{1}[\Omega]=\mathit{cf}_{2}[\Omega]
.
The
\mathit{D}
-loop is called
irreducible
if it does not contain further
\mathit{D}
-loops.
By the pigeonhole principle, any run that is longer than
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}=|Q|\cdot(\mathit{b}+1)^{|\mathit{D}\cup\Omega|}
contains a
\mathit{D}
-loop.
This means the length of an irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loop is at most
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}
.
Moreover, these loops can have at most
2\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}
distinct effects on each counter, where the leading
2
is for the distinction between positive and negative values.
This analysis yields an upper bound of
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{num}}=(2^{|\mathcal{V}|+1}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}})^{d+n}
on the number of irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loops with distinct effects.
We show how to construct a flower as promised in the lemma.

We begin by cutting out irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loops from
\sigma
,
yielding
\sigma^{\prime}
of length at most
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}
.
The extended configuration
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
reached by
\mathit{cf}.\sigma^{\prime}
coincides with
\widehat{\mathit{cf}}
on the
\omega
-entries and on the non-
\omega
-entries for
\mathit{D}
.

Next, we want to shorten
\rho=\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
To this end, we first decompose them into irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loops.
We assume all such
\mathit{D}
-loops have distinct effects, otherwise we pick a representative.
With the previous analysis, the result is
\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\mathit{v}}
with
\mathit{v}\leq\mathit{u}_{\mathit{num}}
.
Here,
\rho_{0}
is the loop on the final state into which the irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loops
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\mathit{v}}
are inserted.
Note that
\rho_{0}
is not necessarily a
\mathit{D}
-loop.
What we know, however, is that
\rho_{0}
has a non-negative effect on the counters in
I
, due to Condition (i) in the definition of inseparability blooms.
Since we want to be able to insert all the
\mathit{D}
-loops directly into
\rho_{0}
, the latter should still contain the same starting configurations for such loops as
\rho
, at least when only considering the counters in
\mathit{D}\cup\Omega
.
Therefore we cannot guarantee that
\rho_{0}
contains no
\mathit{D}
-loops, because cutting all of them out might reduce the number of such configurations visited by
\rho_{0}
.
However,
\rho_{0}
still has length at most
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2}
by the following argument.
If we mark in
\rho_{0}
the first occurrence of each element from
Q\times[0,b]^{\mathit{D}\cup\Omega}
, then each infix leading from one such marker to the next has at most length
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}
, because longer infixes still contain a
\mathit{D}
-loop that has no marked configuration and can therefore be removed.
Since there are at most
|Q\times[0,\mathit{b}]^{\mathit{D}\cup\Omega}|=|Q|\cdot(\mathit{b}+1)^{|\mathit{D}\cup\Omega|}=\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}
markers, we obtain the stated length bound of
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2}
for
\rho_{0}
.

A vector
\bm{x}\in{\rm Nature}^{\mathit{v}}
with
\bm{x}[0]>0
and
\bm{x}[\mathit{j}]\geq 0
for
1\leq j\leq\mathit{v}
can now be turned into a run
\rho_{\bm{x}}
by glueing together
\bm{x}[\mathit{j}]
-many instances of
\rho_{\mathit{j}}
.
Note that also the base loop
\rho_{0}
may be repeated.
As the order of the transitions does not influence the effect of the run,
\delta\varphi(\rho)=\delta\varphi(\rho_{\bm{y}})
holds, where
\bm{y}=\Psi(\rho)
is the so-called Parikh vector that counts the occurrences of irreducible loops in
\rho
.
As a consequence, we can directly define the effect on the vector
\bm{x}
, namely
\delta\varphi(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=0}^{\mathit{v}}\bm{x}[\mathit{j}]\cdot\delta\varphi(\rho_{\mathit{j}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+n}
.

With irreducible
\mathit{D}
-loops at hand, we can formulate the search for a small bloom as an SNLS.
We define the vectors
\bm{x}_{\alpha},\bm{x}_{\beta},\bm{x}_{\gamma}
, and the following constraints:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{\alpha}[0],\bm{x}_{\beta}[0],\bm{x}_{\gamma}[0]\geq

\displaystyle\ 1\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\delta(\bm{x}_{\alpha}+\bm{x}_{\beta}+\bm{x}_{\gamma})[1,d]\geq

\displaystyle\ 0

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{\alpha},\bm{x}_{\beta},\bm{x}_{\gamma}\geq

\displaystyle\ 0

\displaystyle\varphi(\bm{x}_{\alpha}+\bm{x}_{\beta})[d+1,d+n]\geq

\displaystyle\ 0

\displaystyle\delta\varphi(\bm{x}_{\alpha})[I],\delta\varphi(\bm{x}_{\beta})[I],\delta\varphi(\bm{x}_{\gamma})[I]\geq

\displaystyle\ 0

\displaystyle(\varphi(\bm{x}_{\alpha}+\bm{x}_{\beta}+\bm{x}_{\gamma})-\mathit{t}\cdot\varphi(\bm{x}_{\alpha}))[d+1,d+n]=

\displaystyle\ 0

The constraints on the left say that we repeat the base loops at least once, and the remaining loops a non-negative number of times.
The last constraint is Condition (i) in the definition of blooms.
The constraints on the right correspond to the Conditions (ii) to (iv).

It is readily checked that
(\Psi(\alpha),\Psi(\beta),\Psi(\gamma))
solves the SNLS.
This means Theorem
2
applies and yields a small rational solution.
To turn it into an integer solution, we observe that our SNLS is monotonic in the sense that if
(\bm{x}_{\alpha},\bm{x}_{\beta},\bm{x}_{\gamma})
is a solution, so is
(k\bm{x}_{\alpha},k\bm{x}_{\beta},k\bm{x}_{\gamma})
for any
k\geq 1
.
We multiply the rational solution by the common denominator to obtain the integer solution
(\bm{x}_{\alpha}^{\prime},\bm{x}_{\beta}^{\prime},\bm{x}_{\gamma}^{\prime})
with the associated loops
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
.

We argue that
\mathop{\text{---}\!\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1222.42pt{\vbox to1180.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-159.2006pt\lower-1488.80568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lxSVG@eorulefalse\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-rule=nonzero} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}

-1610.63 C 1033.9 -1596.61 1032.8 -1583.33 1030.58 -1570.78 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{\prime}=(q_{f},I,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
is an
\mathit{i}
-inseparability flower.
Remember that
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
is the configuration reached by
\mathit{cf}.\sigma^{\prime}
.
As
\mathit{cf}
and
I
have not changed, the only thing we have to show is the non-negativity for the counters in
\mathit{D}
.
For
\sigma^{\prime}
, this follows from the non-negativity of
\sigma
, and the fact that we only cut-out
\mathit{D}
-loops.
For
\rho^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
, which are executed from
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
, we argue as follows.
The base loop
\rho_{0}
has the same effect on the
\mathit{D}
-counters as
\rho
, because we obtained
\rho_{0}
by cutting-out
\mathit{D}
-loops from
\rho
.
The effect of
\rho
on even the entire set
I
is non-negative due to Condition (i) for blooms.
This means if one repetition of
\rho_{0}
stays non-negative from
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
, arbitrarily many repetitions will.
The one repetition stays non-negative, because
\rho
was non-negative from
\widehat{\mathit{cf}}
(the extended configuration reached by
\mathit{cf}.\sigma
), and
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
coincides with
\widehat{\mathit{cf}}
on
\mathit{D}
.
Since the
\mathit{D}
-loops that we glue into
\rho_{0}
come with a valuation of the counters in
\mathit{D}
, and this valuation keeps them non-negative in
\rho
, the entire
\rho^{\prime}
stays non-negative.

We analyze the complexity of our system
\mathcal{S}
.
It has at most
\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})\in\mathcal{O}(d+n)
many rows, and note that the non-negativity constraints do not count towards the rows.
There are at most
\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{u}_{\mathit{num}})
many columns.
The degree is
\deg(\mathcal{S})=1
.
The maximal coefficient is bounded from above by the largest possible loop effect,

\maxc(\mathcal{S})\leq 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2}
.
Then,
Theorem

2
gives us rational solutions
\bm{x}_{\alpha},\bm{x}_{\beta},\bm{x}_{\gamma}
of the form
\bm{x}_{\rho}[i]=\dfrac{a}{K}
, meaning
K
is the common denominator of all entries, with

\displaystyle|\!|\bm{x}_{\rho}|\!|_{1}\in

\displaystyle\ (\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2}\cdot\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{4})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\mathcal{O}(\mathit{u}_{\mathit{num}})\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{4})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\mathcal{O}((2^{|\mathcal{V}|+1}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}})^{d+n})\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{4})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{5})}=\ (2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot(\mathit{b}+1))^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot\mathit{b})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}\ .

We already argued that the integer vectors
K\bm{x}_{\alpha}
,
K\bm{x}_{\beta}
,
K\bm{x}_{\gamma}
are also solutions with runs
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
.
Since each entry of these vectors is smaller than
(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot\mathit{b})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}
, and we have at most

\mathit{u}_{\mathit{num}}=(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot(\mathit{b}+1))^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}
many loops with maximal size
\mathit{u}_{\mathit{len}}^{2}=|Q|^{2}\cdot(\mathit{b}+1)^{2(d+n)}
, we get

\displaystyle|\rho^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot\mathit{b})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}\cdot(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot(\mathit{b}+1))^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}\cdot|Q|^{2}\cdot(\mathit{b}+1)^{2(d+n)}

\displaystyle=(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|\cdot\mathit{b})^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}\ .\qed

Step III: Rackoff-style induction

We now give the bound on
\mathit{f}(\mathit{i})
that we need to prove
Lemma

14
.
In the base case, no counter has to remain non-negative and so we have a
1
-bounded
0
-inseparability flower.
We employ the bound from Lemma
15
.

Lemma 16
.

\mathit{f}(0)=(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}|Q|)^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}
.

In the induction step, and as in Rackoff’s result, the bound takes the form of a recurrence.

Lemma 17
.

\mathit{f}(i+1)\leq(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{f}(\mathit{i}))^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}
.

Proof.

Consider an
(\mathit{i}+1)
-inseparability flower

-stem for

.

is a loop, it returns to

.
We have
\omega(\mathit{cf})=\omega(\mathit{cf}^{\prime})
and since
\Omega\subseteq\omega(\mathit{cf})
, we get
\Omega\subseteq\omega(\mathit{cf}^{\prime})
.
As

is a bloom,

has a non-negative effect on the counters in

.
This means
\mathit{cf}_{1}[I]\leq\mathit{cf}_{2}[I]
, and so the counters in
([1,\mathit{i}+1]\cap I)\setminus\omega(\mathit{cf}^{\prime})
remain non-negative when executing
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
from
\mathit{cf}_{2}
as they did from
\mathit{cf}_{1}
.
Also
\rho_{2}
remains non-negative from
\mathit{cf}^{\prime}
, because
\rho
remained non-negative from
\mathit{cf}_{1}
.

is actually an

If the counter

that exceeds the bound
\mathit{r}(\mathit{i})
does not belong to
I^{\prime}
, there is nothing to show.
Otherwise, even
\sigma^{\prime}.\alpha^{\prime}.\beta^{\prime}.\gamma^{\prime}
in succession could subtract at most

i+1

\mathit{r}(\mathit{i})
tokens, this leaves us with a positive balance.

remain non-negative.

is an
