\displaystyle\ |Q|\cdot\mathit{r}(\mathit{i})^{\mathit{i}+1}+\mathit{f}(\mathit{i})\leq\ (2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{f}(\mathit{i}))^{\mathcal{O}(\mathit{i}+1)}\leq(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot\mathit{f}(\mathit{i}))^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}\ .\qed

It remains to solve the recurrence.
Let
a=2^{|\mathcal{V}|}
and
b=\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathit{f}(d+n)=(a\ldots(a\cdot\mathit{f}(0))^{b}\ldots)^{b}\leq(a^{d+n}\cdot\mathit{f}(0))^{b^{d+n}}\ .

Since
\mathit{f}(0)=(2^{|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot|Q|)^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{6})}
, we obtain the promised
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathit{f}(d+n)\leq 2^{|\mathcal{V}|^{\mathcal{O}((d+n)^{2})}}
.

Appendix A
Additional material on Section
4

We continue our notation
n=\mathit{col}(\bm{A}(x))
and
m=\mathit{row}(\bm{A}(x))
from the main paper.
In contexts like
\deg(-)
,
\maxc(-)
, etc. we ommit the parameter
x
where convenient.
For example we write
\deg(\bm{A})
instead of
\deg(\bm{A}(x))
.

A.1
Precise bounds for Theorem
5

Here, we prove that the DNFLB
\Phi
constructed in
Equation

4
satisfies the bounds promised in
Theorem

5
.

To that end, it suffices to compute for all
R
the maximal degrees and maximal coefficients of the terms
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
,
\bm{D}(x)\cdot\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(x)
, and
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}(x)
.
Let
d_{R,i,j}(x)
be the entry in the
i
-th row and
j
-th column of
\bm{D}_{R}(x)
.
Further let
[1,n]=P\uplus Q
where
Q
is the set of row indices
i<n
for which a
j_{i}<n
exists with
d_{R,i,j_{i}}=1
and
d_{R,i,j}=0
for all
j\neq j_{i}
.
We construct
\bm{D}_{R}(x)
by first augmenting
\bm{A}
by
n
rows of the form
(\ldots,1,\ldots)
with exactly one non-zero component, and then choosing
|R|=n
rows.
This implies
|P|\leq m
.
We recall Leibniz’ formula for computing the determinant
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sgn(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{R,i,\sigma(i)}
.
Define
T=\{\sigma\in S_{n}\;|\;\forall i\in Q.\;\sigma(i)=j_{i}\}
, where
j_{i}
is the non-zero valued column of the
i
-th row in
\bm{D}_{R}
.
We observe that if
\sigma\in S_{n}
with
\sigma(i)\neq j_{i^{\prime}}
for some
i^{\prime}\in Q
, then
\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{R,i,\sigma(i)}=0
because it contains the operand
d_{R,i^{\prime},j_{i^{\prime}}}=0
.
Then,

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sgn(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{R,i,\sigma(i)}=\sum_{\sigma\in T}\sgn(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}d_{R,i,\sigma(i)}=\sum_{\sigma\in T}\sgn(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i\in P}d_{R,i,\sigma(i)}.

The latter equality follows from the fact that
\sigma(i)=j_{i}
for all
\sigma\in T
and
i\in Q
, where we have
d_{R,i,j_{i}}=1
by definition of
Q
.
The formula shows
\deg(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\leq|P|\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\leq m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
.
Because
\sigma(i)=j_{i}
for all
\sigma\in S_{n}
and
i\in Q
, this fixes the images of all but
|P|
inputs.
Since
\sigma\in T
are permutations, we can conclude
|T|=|P|!\leq m!
.
Regarding the maximal coefficient, note that each polynomial
d_{R,i,j}(x)
is an additive term of at most
\deg(\bm{D}_{R}(x))+1
monomials, each with a potentially large coefficient.
Therefore, Leibniz’ formula and the above equality yield

\displaystyle\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))~

\displaystyle\leq m!\cdot\big((\deg(\bm{D}_{R}(x))+1)\cdot\maxc(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\big)^{|P|}

\displaystyle\leq m!\cdot\big((\deg(\bm{D}_{R}(x))+1)\cdot\maxc(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\big)^{m}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m\log_{2}m}\cdot(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})+1)^{m}\cdot(\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{m}

\displaystyle\leq(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}.

Recall that each entry of the adjugate
\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
can be computed by deleting one row and one column from
\bm{D}_{R}(x)
and computing the determinant of the resulting matrix.
This implies
\deg(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\leq\deg(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))
.
We cannot conclude
\maxc(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\leq\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))
, because terms with conflicting signs in the Leibniz formula might negate each other in
\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
, while not doing so in
\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))
.
However, the approximation we do above for
\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))
assumes that all summands have the same sign, and therefore is also an upperbound for
\maxc(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))
.
We have

\displaystyle\deg(\bm{D}(x)\cdot\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(x))~

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})+m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(m+2)\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))\ .

For the maximal coefficient, we again remark that each polynomial consists of at most
\deg+1
monomials,
and further that computing each entry per matrix multiplication here involves adding up
n
terms.

Thus, we get

\displaystyle\maxc(\bm{D}(x)\cdot\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}_{R}(x))

\displaystyle~\begin{aligned} \leq~&(\deg(\bm{D}(x))\cdot\maxc(\bm{D}(x)))\cdot n\cdot(\deg(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\cdot\maxc(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x))))\\
&\cdot n\cdot(\deg(\bm{c}_{R}(x))\cdot\maxc(\bm{c}_{R}(x)))\\
\leq~&(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{2}\cdot n^{2}\cdot(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))\cdot(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\\
=~&(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\ .\end{aligned}

\deg(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}(x))\leq m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})+\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))
,

\displaystyle\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))\cdot\bm{c}(x))

\displaystyle~\leq(\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\cdot\deg(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x))))\cdot m\cdot(\maxc(\bm{c}(x))\cdot\deg(\bm{c}(x)))

\displaystyle~\leq(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})^{2}\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle~=(\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\ .

Since
\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\deg(\mathcal{S})
and
\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\maxc(\mathcal{S})
by construction,
we obtain the required values for polynomials appearing in
\Phi(x)
.

A.2
Precise bounds for Lemma
4

In this subsection, we provide a proof of
Lemma

4
that yields the precise bounds promised in the statement.

With a DNFLB
\Phi(x)
in hand, we now establish a bound on the size of a smallest solution.
To this end we apply a root separation theorem that gives a lower bound on the distance of distinct real roots of a polynomial.
We define the
real roots
of a polynomial
p(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
as

\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p(x))=\{-\infty,\infty\}\cup\{r\in\mathbb{R}\;|\;p(r)=0\}
.
The advantage of including
-\infty
and
\infty
is that the entire set
\mathbb{R}
decomposes into intervals between adjacent roots of
p(x)
, which will save us case distinctions.
Thus, recall that the number of (even complex) roots of a polynomial of degree
d
is at most
d+2
.
For a DNFLB
\Phi(x)
, we define
\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(x))
as the set of all real roots among its polynomials.
Finally, we define
\mathsf{Separation}(p(x))=\min_{r\neq r^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p(x))}|r-r^{\prime}|
as the minimal distance between two distinct real roots.
We restate Rump’s bound from the main paper.
See
7

We now formulate the feasibility of
\Phi(x)
in
terms of constraints on real roots.
Consider one of its lower bound constraints
p(x)>0
.
Let
-\infty=r_{1}<r_{2}<\ldots<r_{k}=\infty
be the real roots of
p(x)
.
The key observation is that, on the entire interval
(r_{i},r_{i+1})
, the polynomial
p(x)
is either positive or negative.
We evaluate up-front which is the case, and collect in the set
P\subseteq\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p(x))
the roots
r_{i}
where a positive interval starts.
Then the lower bound constraint
p(x)>0
is equivalent to
\bigvee_{r_{i}\in P}r_{i}<x<r_{i+1}
.
We refer to a formula of the form
\ell<x<u
as an
interval constraint
, and call
\ell
a lower and
u
an upper bound.

Consider a conjunction of interval constraints
\bigwedge_{i\in I}\ell_{i}<x<u_{i}
with
I
a finite index set.
A rational number
\mathit{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
will only satisfy this conjunction if it is larger than the largest lower bound
\ell=\max_{i\in I}\ell_{i}
, and smaller than the least upper bound
u=\min_{i\in I}u_{i}
.
This means the conjunction is equivalent to the single interval constraint
\ell<x<u
.

To deal with lower bound constraints
p(x)\geq 0
that are not strict, we
generalize interval constraints to admit both relations,
<
and
\leq
.
To generalize the elimination of conjunctions, these relations can also be mixed, so we also consider
\ell\leq x<u
an interval constraint.
We refer to a disjunction of interval constraints as a DINC
\Theta(x)
.
We use
\mathsf{Bounds}(\Theta(x))
for the set of bounds that appear in
\Theta(x)
.
The discussion yields the following.

Lemma 18
.

For every DNFLB
\Phi(x)
, there is a DINC
\Theta(x)
that satisfies
\Phi(x)\mathop{\models\hskip-8.5359pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\models$}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\Theta(x)
and
\mathsf{Bounds}(\Theta(x))\subseteq\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(x))
.

With this, we are finally ready to prove
Lemma

4
.

Proof of
Lemma

4
.

Consider an SNLS
\mathcal{S}(x,\bm{y})=\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)\wedge\bm{y}\geq 0
that is feasible (over the rationals).
Let
\bm{A}(x)
be an
m\times n
matrix.
We first apply
Theorem

5
to obtain the DNFLB
\Phi(x)
whose polynomials have a degree bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))
and a maximal coefficient bounded by
n^{2}\cdot(\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}
.
We then apply
Lemma

18
to turn this DNFLB into a DINC
\Theta(x)
.

Since the SNLS is feasible and the two transformations yield formulae that are equivalent over the rationals, there is an interval constraint
\ell<x<u
in
\Theta(x)
that has a rational solution.
We show that there is even a rational
\mathit{t}=\frac{a}{b}
with
\ell<\mathit{t}<u

that satisfies the bound promised by
Lemma

4
.
We proceed by a case distinction along
\ell
and
u
.

Case
\bm{\ell<u}
:

In the special case
\ell=-\infty
and
u=\infty
, we pick
a=0
and
b=1
as a solution.
Assume
u\neq\infty
, the case
\ell\neq-\infty
is similar.
Then
\ell\in\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{1})
and
u\in\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{2})
for two polynomials in
\Phi(x)
.
Note that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
may be different.
However,
\ell
and
u
are both roots of the product polynomial
p_{\times}=p_{1}\cdot p_{2}
, meaning

\ell,u\in\mathsf{Roots}_{\mathbb{R}}(p_{\times})
.
Note that
\deg(p_{\times})=\deg(p_{1})+\deg(p_{2})\in\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))
and

|\!|p_{\times}|\!|_{1}\leq|\!|p_{1}|\!|_{1}\cdot|\!|p_{2}|\!|_{1}
.
Therefore using Rump’s Bound we get

\displaystyle|u-\ell|

\displaystyle~>~

\displaystyle\big(\deg(p_{\times})^{\deg(p_{\times})+1}\cdot(1+|p_{\times}|_{1})^{2\deg(p_{\times})}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle~\geq~

\displaystyle\left(\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}\\
&\cdot\Big(\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))\cdot(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\Big)^{\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}\end{aligned}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle~\geq~

\displaystyle(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{-\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}.

In the following, we use
B
as a shorthand for

(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}
.
Immediately, we get
B(u-\ell)>1
.
Thus, there is an integer
a\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
B\ell<Bu-1\leq a<Bu
.
Dividing by
B
yields
\ell<\frac{a}{B}<u
, meaning we can choose a
denominator
b\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
for a suitable solution with
b\leq B
.
Let
u
be a root of
p_{1}
.
We apply Cauchy’s bound Lemma
9
on root sizes,
and get

|u|\leq 1+|p_{1}|_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))\cdot(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})},

and therefore
|a|\leq B|u|+1\leq B\cdot(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}
.
Due to the
\mathcal{O}
-notation, the second factor is subsumed by
B
here.

Case
\bm{\ell=u}
:

Then
\frac{a}{b}=\ell=u
, and this is a root of a polynomial
p(x)
in
\Phi(x)
.
Thus, there is a polynomial
p
in
\Phi(x)
such that
\frac{a}{b}\in\mathsf{Roots}(p)
.
Since also
\frac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}
we get that
a,b\leq\maxc(p)

using the Rational Root Theorem (Theorem
8
).
This implies
a,b\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}
.

In all cases
B=(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}

is a bound on the complexity of
x
, as required.
∎

A.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We can now put the ingredients together and show
Theorem

2
.
We write
n=\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})=\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
and
m=\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})
, as in the previous subsections.
According to
Lemma

4
, we know that if our system

\mathcal{S}:=\bm{A}(x)\cdot\bm{y}\geq\bm{b}(x)\wedge\bm{y}\geq 0

is feasible with some
(t,\bm{s})\in\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, then for some
t\in\mathbb{Q}
with

|\!|t|\!|_{1}\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}.

We can now apply the argument from
Theorem

5
again: We have shown there that if there exists a solution
\bm{s}
for a particular
t
with
\bm{A}(t)\cdot\bm{s}\geq\bm{b}(t)
and
\bm{s}\geq\bm{0}
, then for some
R\subseteq[1,m+n]
, for which
\bm{D}_{R}(t)
is invertible, the following
\bm{s}^{*}
is a solution to
\mathcal{S}
:

\bm{s}^{*}=\bm{D}_{R}^{-1}(t)\cdot c_{R}(t)=\frac{\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(t))}{\det(\bm{D}_{R}(t))}\cdot c_{R}(t).

Moreover, in the proof of
Theorem

5
, we have seen that

\displaystyle\deg(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\deg(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\maxc(\det(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}

\displaystyle\maxc(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}

\displaystyle|\!|\adj(D_{R}(t))|\!|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\deg(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\cdot\maxc(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))\cdot|\!|t|\!|_{1}^{\deg(\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(x)))}

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\cdot|\!|t|\!|_{1}^{m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\cdot|\!|t|\!|_{1}^{m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{2})}\cdot\left((n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc\mathcal{S})^{\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}\right)^{m\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}

Since for
\det(D_{R}(x))
, we have the same bounds on maximal coefficients and degree as for
\adj(D_{R}(x))
, we can show similarly:

\displaystyle|\!|\det(\bm{D}_{R}(t))|\!|_{1}\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}

Moreover, clearly:

|\!|c_{R}(t)|\!|_{1}\leq\maxc(\mathcal{S})\cdot|\!|t|\!|_{1}\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc\mathcal{S})^{\mathcal{O}(m^{3}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S}))}.

Furthermore, we have

|\!|\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(t))\cdot c_{R}(t)|\!|_{1}\leq n\cdot(|\!|\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(t))|\!|_{1}+|\!|c_{R}(t)|\!|_{1})\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}.

Therefore, we can upper bound
\bm{s}^{*}
as follows:

\displaystyle|\!|\bm{s}^{*}|\!|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(t))}{\det(\bm{D}_{R}(t))}\cdot c_{R}(t)\right\|_{1}\leq|\!|\adj(\bm{D}_{R}(t))\cdot c_{R}(t)|\!|_{1}+|\!|\det(\bm{D}_{R}(t))|\!|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}+(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(n\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(m^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}

As we mentioned in the main paper, if we can ensure that all components of
c_{R}(t)
have the same denominator, we also ensure this for
\bm{s}^{*}
.
To avoid clutter, we did not argue this property in the bound above.
However, this does not change the bound.
In order to ensure that the components of
c_{R}(t)
all have the same denominator, we only need to multiply the denominator and nominator of each component by a factor of at most
q^{\deg(\mathcal{S})}
where
q
is the denominator of
t
.
This is because the components of
c_{R}(t)
are obtained by summing integer multiples of
1,t^{1},\ldots,t^{\deg(\mathcal{S})}
.
The bit size of
c_{R}(t)
, and thus the resulting
\bm{s}^{*}
, blows up by a factor of at most
\deg(\mathcal{S})
, which is absorbed by the term
(\mathit{col}(\mathcal{S})\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})\cdot\maxc(\mathcal{S}))^{\mathcal{O}(\mathit{row}(\mathcal{S})^{4}\cdot\deg(\mathcal{S})^{2})}
.
This completes the proof of
Theorem

2
.

Appendix B
Additional material on Section
5

In this section we show Lemma
12
.
To do this, we use the inseparability characterization given in

[
2
]
.
We break Lemma
12
down into its two directions.

Lemma 19
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible VASS.
If there is a coverable inseparability flower
(\mathit{cf}.\sigma,\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1222.42pt{\vbox to1180.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-159.2006pt\lower-1488.80568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

.

For Lemma
19
, we use the
basic separator characterization of regular separability in
[
2
]
.
Given a Büchi VASS
\mathcal{V}
, regular separability of its language
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})
from
D_{n}
is characterized in terms of inclusion in a finite union of so-called basic separators.
The authors identify two families of languages that together make up the set of basic separators,

\{P_{i,k}\;|\;i\in[1,n],k\in{\rm Nature}\}
and
\{S_{\bm{x},k}\;|\;\bm{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{n},k\in{\rm Nature}\}
.
We will define these families below. Let us first formally state the characterization.

Lemma 21

(
[
2
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible VASS.
Then,
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathrel{|}D_{n}
if and only if there is a number
k\in{\rm Nature}
and a finite set of vectors
X\subseteq{\rm Nature}^{n}
such that

\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}P_{i,k}\cup\bigcup_{\bm{x}\in X}S_{\bm{x},k}\ .

Both language families impose conditions on the letter balance along a word.
Given a word
w\in\Sigma_{n}^{*}
its
letter balance

\varphi(w)
is a vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, whose
i
-th component
\varphi_{i}(w)
counts how many more letters
a_{i}
than
\bar{a}_{i}
appear in
w
.
In other words this balance is equal to the effect on the external counters of a finite run
\mathit{cf}.\rho
that is labeled by the word
w
, i.e.
\varphi(w)=\varphi(\rho)
.

The language
P_{i,k}\subseteq\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}
contains words whose balance is bounded by
k
in the
i
-th component before becoming negative for some prefix.
In contrast, the language
S_{\bm{x},k}\subseteq\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}
is prefix independent.
It contains words whose
\bm{x}
-weighted balance gradually decreases beyond some point, while all suffixes beyond the same point have an
\bm{x}
-weighted balance bounded by
k
.
Here the
\bm{x}
-weighted balance
of a word
v\in\Sigma_{n}^{*}
for some vector
\bm{x}\in{\rm Nature}^{n}
is the dot product
\langle\bm{x},\varphi(v)\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.

These language families are defined formally below, for all
i\in[1,n]
,
\bm{x}\in{\rm Nature}^{n}
, and
k\in{\rm Nature}
.
For a word
w\in\Sigma_{n}^{*}
or
w\in\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}
, we use
\mathsf{prefix}(w)
to denote the set of finite prefixes of
w
, and
\mathsf{infix}(w)
to denote the set of finite infixes of
w
.

\displaystyle P_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}\;|\;\exists v\in\mathsf{prefix}(w).\varphi_{i}(v)<0\wedge\forall u\in\mathsf{prefix}(v).\varphi_{i}(u)\leq k\}

\displaystyle S_{\bm{x},k}

\displaystyle=\{w.v\in\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}\;|\;\forall u\in\mathsf{infix}(v).\langle\bm{x},\varphi(u)\rangle\leq k\;\;\wedge\;\;v=v_{0}.v_{1}\ldots,\forall j\in{\rm Nature}.\;\langle\bm{x},\varphi(v_{j})\rangle<0\}

With this at hand, we show Lemma
19
.
We recall the argument in
[
2
, Proposition 5.5]
and extend it to include the basic separators of the form
P_{i,k}
for all
i\in[1,n]
,
k\in{\rm Nature}
.

Proof of Lemma
19
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible VASS, and let

-1725.76 C 556.8 -1779.63 577.47 -1827.23 618.79 -1868.56 C 660.86 -1909.14 708.83 -1929.44 762.7 -1929.44 C 838.71 -1929.44 903.65 -1894.75 957.52 -1825.38 C 1008.44 -1760.44 1033.9 -1688.86 1033.9 -1610.63 C 1033.9 -1596.61 1032.8 -1583.33 1030.58 -1570.78 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par})
is coverable,
(q_{f},\bm{m})
is coverable as well, hence there must be witnessing runs covering the non-
\omega
counters of
(q_{f},\bm{m})
and reaching arbitrarily high values for the counters in
\omega(q_{f},\bm{m})\supseteq\Omega
.
In particular, there must be some run
(q_{0},\bm{0}).\rho
that covers the configuration
(q_{f},\bm{m}^{\prime})
.

We know that upon taking
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
from
(q_{f},\bm{m})
, the counter
i
remains positive and the counter valuation does not decrease, for all
i\in I
.
Clearly this remains true if we repeat these loops
(k+1)
-times, i.e. taking
\alpha^{k+1}
,
\beta^{k+1}
, and
\gamma^{k+1}
.
By our choice of
y
, we also know that starting from some counter valuation
u\in{\rm Nature}
for counter
i\in[1,d+n]
, during execution of
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
we always remain above the valuation
u-y
for this counter.
Therefore, since
\bm{m}^{\prime}
has a counter valuation of
3\cdot(k+1)\cdot y
for all counters in
\Omega\subseteq\omega(q_{f},\bm{m})
, taking
\alpha^{k+1}.\beta^{k+1}.\gamma^{k+1}
from
(q_{f},\bm{m}^{\prime})
keeps the counters in
\Omega
positive as well.
So, we deduce that the run
(q_{0},\bm{0}).\rho.\alpha^{k+1}.\beta^{k+1}.\gamma^{k+1}
remains positive in all counters
[1,d+n]
.
Furthermore condition (ii) for an inseparability bloom,
\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta)+\delta(\gamma)\geq 0
, implies that
\alpha^{k+1}.\beta^{k+1}.\gamma^{k+1}
can be infinitely repeated to produce a run that remains positive in the counters
\{1,\ldots d\}
.
Because
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are loops on the final state
q_{f}
, the run
(q_{0},\bm{0}).\rho.(\alpha^{k+1}.\beta^{k+1}.\gamma^{k+1})^{\omega}
is an accepting run in
\mathcal{V}
.
Let
w\in\Sigma_{n}^{\omega}
be the word labeling this run.
The argument in the proof of
[
2
, Proposition 5.5]
applies to show
w\not\in S_{\bm{x},k}
for all
\bm{x}\in X
.

It remains to argue that
w\not\in P_{i,k}
for all
i<n
.
If
d+i\in I
, then we know that the counter
d+i
never becomes negative along the run, and thus the same holds for the
i
th component of the letter balance.
Then
w\not\in P_{i,k}
.
If
d+i\in\Omega
, then taking
\rho
from
(q_{0},\bm{0})
covers
(q_{f},\bm{m}^{\prime})
, where
\bm{m}^{\prime}[d+i]\geq k+1
by definition.
Therefore the
i
th letter balance exceeds bound
k+1
before it can drop below
0
, so
w\not\in P_{i,k}
.
∎

For the direction of Lemma
20
, we do not directly argue from an assumption of inseparability.
Instead, we use another characterization provided in
[
2
, Theorem 5.3]
.
This characterization follows in two steps.
First, it is shown that a Büchi VASS
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}
can be constructed from
\mathcal{V}
such that the language
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
is (so-called)
pumpable
, and
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathrel{|}D_{n}
if and only if
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})\mathrel{|}D_{n}
.
The definition of pumpability is not needed for the purposes of this paper.

For our proof, we only need the second step of the characterization: If
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})
is pumpable, then
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathrel{\not|}D_{n}
if and only if the Büchi Automaton
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V})
contains a structure called a
KM-inseparability flower
1
1

1

In
[
2
]
this structure is simply called
inseparability flower
. Since we already use this term for a different structure, which does not involve the KM-graph, we felt it appropriate to add the prefix “KM-” for the version from
[
2
]
, to differentiate the two.
.
We define these kinds of flowers and the notation
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V})
below.
Putting the two steps together, we get the following characterization.

Lemma 22

(
[
2
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible VASS.
Then,
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathrel{\not|}D_{n}
if and only if
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
contains a KM-inseparability flower.

We recall the definitions we need.
For the rest of this section, fix a Büchi VASS
\mathcal{V}=(Q,q_{0},\Sigma_{n},T,F)
.
We start with the definition of the Karp-Miller graph
[
15
]
of a VASS, as formulated in
[
2
]
.
The
Karp-Miller graph

\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V})=(Q_{\mathsf{KM}},(q_{0},\bm{0}),T,T_{\mathsf{KM}},F\times{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d})
is a Büchi Automaton with states
Q_{\mathsf{KM}}\subseteq Q\times{\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d}
, whose transitions are labeled with the transitions of
\mathcal{V}
.
Its states and transitions are constructed by starting with
Q_{\mathsf{KM}}=\emptyset
and
T_{\mathsf{KM}}=\{(q_{0},\bm{0})\}
, and repeating the following steps towards a fixed point.
Pick a transition
t=(q,a,\bm{x},q^{\prime})\in T
and a state
(q,\bm{m})\in T_{\mathsf{KM}}
.
First simulate
(q,a,\bm{x},q^{\prime})
from
(q,\bm{m})
, to get
(q^{\prime},\bm{m}+\bm{x})
.
If
(q^{\prime},\bm{m}+\bm{x})\notin Q\times{\rm Nature}^{d+n}_{\omega}
, continue.
In the other case construct
\bm{m}_{\omega}
from
\bm{m}+\bm{x}
as follows:
(1) for
i\in[1,d]
, if there is a state
(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime}),\in Q_{\mathsf{KM}}
with
\bm{m}^{\prime}\leq\bm{m}+\bm{x}
and
\bm{m}^{\prime}[i]<(\bm{m}+\bm{x})[i]
such that
(q,\bm{m})
is reachable from
(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})
using the transitions generated so far, then set
\bm{m}_{\omega}[i]=\omega
;
(2) otherwise, set
\bm{m}_{\omega}[i]=(\bm{m}+\bm{x})[i]
.
Finally set
Q_{\mathsf{KM}}:=Q_{\mathsf{KM}}\cup\{(q^{\prime},\bm{m}_{\omega})\}
and
T_{\mathsf{KM}}:=T_{\mathsf{KM}}\cup\{(q,\bm{m})\xrightarrow{t}(q^{\prime},\bm{m}_{\omega})\}
.
This process reaches a fixed point after finitely many states, since
({\rm Nature}_{\omega}^{d},\leq)
is a well quasi order.
In particular, this means that the state space of the Karp Miller graph is finite.
It is immediate from the definition that if
(q,\bm{m})\in Q\times{\rm Nature}^{d}_{\omega}
can be reached in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V})
, then
(q,\bm{m})
is coverable in
\mathcal{V}
.

Now we define
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}
.
The VASS
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}=(Q_{\mathsf{pump}},q_{0},\Sigma_{n},T_{\mathsf{pump}},F\times{\rm Nature}^{d+n}_{\omega})
has the states
Q_{\mathsf{KM}}
of the Karp-Miller graph
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
as its state space, and
((q,\bm{m}),a,\bm{x},(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime}))\in T_{\mathsf{pump}}
if and only if
(q,\bm{m})\xrightarrow{t}(q^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})
is a transition in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
for some
t=(q,a,\bm{x},q^{\prime})
.

A KM-inseparability flower in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
consists of a reachable final state
(q_{f},\bm{m})
in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
with three loops
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
on that state that fulfill the following three conditions.

[(a)]

1.

\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta)+\delta(\gamma)\geq\bm{0}
,

2.

\varphi(\alpha)+\varphi(\beta)\geq\bm{0}
,

3.

there is
t\in\mathbb{Q}
with
\varphi(\alpha)+\varphi(\beta)+\varphi(\gamma)-t\cdot\varphi(\alpha)=\bm{0}
.

Here, for a path (or cycle)
\rho
in the Karp-Miller graph, we write
\delta(\rho)
to refer to the effect of the transition sequence it was labelled by.
In other words for all
\chi\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
labelled by a sequence of transitions
\rho_{\chi}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}
, we define
\delta(\chi):=\delta(\rho_{\chi})
.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
20
.

Proof of Lemma
20
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an
n
-visible VASS with
\mathit{L}(\mathcal{V})\mathrel{\not|}D_{n}
.
Then by
Lemma

22

\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
contains a KM-inseparability flower.
This means that there is a state
(q_{f},\bm{m})
of
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}})
and there are three loops
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
on this state with the properties (a), (b), (c).
Recall that the state space of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}
is that of
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
.
Also recall that
((q,\bm{n}),a,\bm{x},(q^{\prime},\bm{n}^{\prime}))\in T_{\mathsf{pump}}
if and only if there is a transition
(q,\bm{n})\xrightarrow{t}(q^{\prime},\bm{n}^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
for some
t=(q,a,\bm{x},q^{\prime})\in T
.
Therefore
q_{f}=(q_{f}^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime})
is a state in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
, for some
\bm{m}^{\prime}\in{\rm Nature}^{d+n}_{\omega}
and final
q_{f}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{V}
with three loops
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
on it in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
that fulfill (a), (b), and (c).
Here
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
are obtained by extracting the
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{pump}}
-transitions from the labels of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and then matching them to the corresponding transitions in
\mathsf{KM}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n})
.
Since
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
are themselves labelled by transition sequences (of
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{D}_{n}
), we can extract again to obtain the loops
\alpha_{\mathcal{V}}
,
\beta_{\mathcal{V}}
,
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}
in
\mathcal{V}
(ignoring the
\mathcal{D}_{n}
-components of the transitions).
We claim that
((q_{f}^{\prime},\bm{m}^{\prime}).\varepsilon,\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1222.42pt{\vbox to1180.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-159.2006pt\lower-1488.80568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\par}=(q_{f}^{\prime},I,\alpha_{\mathcal{V}},\beta_{\mathcal{V}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}})
